Long time validity of the linearized Boltzmann uncut-off and the linearized Landau equations from the Newton Law
Corentin Le Bihan

Abstract
. We provide a rigorous justification of the linearized Boltzmann and Landau equations for interacting particle systems with long-range interaction. The result shows that for a system of N N Hamiltonian particles governed by truncated power law potentials of the form 𝒰 ε eff ​ ( r ) ∼ | r / ε eff | − s \mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}(r)\sim|r/{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}|^{-s} near r ≈ 0 r\approx 0 (with ε eff {\varepsilon_{\rm eff}} the effective radius of the particles ), the covariance of the equilibrium fluctuations converges to solutions of kinetic equations in appropriate scaling limits ε eff → 0 \varepsilon_{\rm eff}\rightarrow 0 and N → ∞ N\rightarrow\infty , corresponding to a low density regime . We prove that in dimension 3 3 , for s ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) s\in(1,\infty) , the limiting system approaches the uncutoff linearized Boltzmann equation for the scaling N ​ ε eff 2 = 1 N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=1 . The Coulomb singularity s = 1 s=1 appears as a threshold value. Kinetic scaling limits with s ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] s\in(0,1] universally converge to the linearized Landau equation, and we prove the onset of the Coulomb logarithm for s = 1 s=1 .



1.
Introduction

In kinetic theory, a gas of particles can be modeled by a large system of
N
classical particles interacting
via
a potential
\mathscr{U}(\cdot/{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}})
, with
{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}
the
effective radius
of a particle
. The dynamics is Hamiltonian and obeys the Newton laws with assigned potential
\mathscr{U}
.
In dimension
3
, the power laws
\mathscr{U}_{s}(r):=r^{-s}
,
s\geq 1
play a fundamental role, in particular the Coulomb case
s=1
. One of the goals of kinetic theory is the description of such a gas in the limit
N\to\infty
,
{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}\to 0
. Of course, the asymptotic behavior
depends on the relation between
{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}
and
N
. Collisional kinetic theory concerns low density scalings, where the occupied volume
N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{3}
goes to
0
. The good scaling limit looks to be the Boltzmann–Grad limit
N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=1
, at least for
s>1
(see
[
Gra49
]
). It corresponds to a regime such that on a time interval of length
1
, any particle passes nearby another particle (at distance less than
{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}
, see Remark
1.1
for a comment on this scaling).

In the case
s>1
, if at time 0 the particles are "sufficiently independent", the distribution of a typical particle
f(t,x,v)
is a solution of the Boltzmann equation (introduced by Maxwell in 1867
[
Max03
]
and Boltzmann in 1871
[
Bol96
]
)

(1.1)

\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=Q_{s}(f,f)\\
Q_{s}(f,h)(v):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\left(f(v^{\prime})h(v_{*}^{\prime})-f(v)h(v_{*})\right)b_{s}(v-v_{*},\eta)\,\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\,\mathrm{d}{\eta},\\
v^{\prime}=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}+\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\eta,\penalty\ v_{*}^{\prime}=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}-\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\eta,\end{array}

where the kernel
b_{s}
depends on the potential
\mathscr{U}_{s}(\cdot)
. The collision operator
Q
can be interpreted as a jump operator for the velocities. The rigorous proof of such a theorem for long range interactions is now an open problem, and this paper can be interpreted as an intermediate result.

For a power law
\mathscr{U}_{s}
with
s\geq 1
, the kernel
b_{s}
is equal to

(1.2)

b_{s}(z,\eta)=|z|^{\frac{s-4}{s}}q_{s}(z\cdot\eta),\penalty\ {\rm with}\penalty\ q_{s}(\cos\theta)\underset{\theta\sim 0}{\sim}K\theta^{-\frac{2+s}{s}}

for some constant
K
. Hence, the collision kernel is not integrable near the singularity
\eta\cdot\tfrac{v-v_{*}}{|v-v_{*}|}\simeq 1
(when the collisions are grazing). We say that the Boltzmann kernel has no cutoff. However, the Boltzmann operator
Q_{s}
can be defined (everywhere) for
s>1

if the functions
f
and
h
are differentiable (see
[
Gou97
,
Vil98
]
).

In the Coulombian case
s=1
, the singularity near
\theta\sim 0
is too large to define the collision operator, even for smooth functions. In 1936, Landau proposed in
[
Lan36
]
an operator that describes the collision between Coulombian particles. His analysis was based on cutting the long range interaction, responsible for the "collisions" with small deviation angle
\theta
. His argument was that in a neutral Coulomb gas, the interactions between ions (big positively charged particles) are screened by electrons (small negatively charged particles). Hence, the particles interact
via
the effective potential
\tfrac{\chi(|x|/R)}{|x|}
where
\chi\simeq\mathbbm{1}_{[0,1]}
is a cutting function, and
R
is called the Debye radius
1
1

1

In the Physics literature the constant
R
is typically interpreted as a normalisation constant, to be estimated
. In the present paper we study the limit
R\to\infty
. (
R\gg 1
).

Landau finally obtained the collision operator
c(\log R)Q_{L}(f,f)
where
c
is a diffusion coefficient,
\log R
is the
Coulomb logarithm
coming from the singularity, and
Q_{L}
is defined by

(1.3)

Q_{L}(f,h)(v):=2\pi\nabla_{v}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{|v-v_{*}|^{2}{\rm Id}-(v-v_{*})^{\otimes 2}}{|v-v_{*}|^{3}}\big(\nabla f(v)h(v_{*})-f(v)\nabla h(v_{*})\big)\,\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}.

The Landau equation is

(1.4)

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=cQ_{L}(f,f).

Remark 1.1
.

It is not clear if, in the case
s\leq 2
, the Boltzmann equation is really the good description in the Boltzmann-Grad limit
N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=1
of the particle system with interaction through the long range potential
\mathscr{U}_{s}(\tfrac{x}{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}})=\tfrac{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{s}}{|x|^{s}}
.

Indeed, it can be compared with the mean-field regime
N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}={\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2-s}\ll 1
(the interaction potential is
\tfrac{1}{N}\mathscr{U}_{s}(x)
), which is much more dilute. Then, the mean-field effects should dominate the collisional effects. One can expect that, because of some "screening effect" (similar to that in the Coulomb case), particles interact via an effective potential with faster decay at infinity.

For hard potentials
s>2
, the Boltzmann-Grad scaling is dominant, and the Boltzmann equation should be the true asymptotic description of the system. A similar discussion has been performed in
[
SW25
]
for the definition of the equilibrium measure for particles interacting through the potential
\mathscr{U}_{s}(\tfrac{\cdot}{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}})
.

In this paper we will work with microscopically truncated interactions and therefore avoid the discussion of screening properties. Our purpose, however, is to obtain uncutoff equations by removing the truncation.

The first rigorous derivation of the Boltzmann equation was performed by Lanford for hard-spheres
[
Lan75
]
and by King
[
Kin75
]
for regular potentials with compact support.

Our strategy for deriving the Boltzmann (or Landau) equation associated with the potential
\mathscr{U}_{s}
is to split the problem into two steps. First, we consider a system of particles interacting
via

the screened
potential

\mathscr{U}_{s,R}(\tfrac{x}{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}}):=\chi(\tfrac{|x|}{R{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}})\tfrac{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{s}}{|x|^{s}},

where
R
is a cutoff radius (which will go to infinity),

\chi(r):\mathbb{R}^{+}\to[0,1]
is a smooth, decreasing cutoff function:

\chi(0)=1,\penalty\ \chi([1,\infty[)=\{0\},\penalty\ \chi^{\prime}\leq 0.

Taking the Boltzmann-Grad limit
N\to\infty
,
N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=1
(
N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=(\log R)^{-1}
in the Coulomb case
s=1
), we recover the cut-off Boltzmann equation. Defining a "collision" between two particles as the moment when they get closer than the effective radius
{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}
, the Boltzmann-Grad scaling is chosen such that a particle has on average one collision per unite of time.

In a second time, we take the grazing collision limit
R\to\infty
to pass from the cut-off Boltzmann equation to the Boltzmann equation associated with
\mathscr{U}_{s}
if
s>1
(respectively, the Landau equation if
s=1
).

If we obtain some quantitative estimates for the two regimes, one can hope to take the limits
R\to\infty,N\to\infty
simultaneously, assuming that
R
grows much slower than
N
(we will need
R
of order
O((\log\log N)^{1/6})
).

The big difficulty is that we need the validity of the cut-off Boltzmann equation on a large time interval of order
O(1)
(to be compared to the validity time
O(R^{-2})
obtained by King
[
Kin75
]
for interaction potentials supported in a ball of radius
R
, see the next section). In order to get a long-time result, we look at a linearized version of the system near the thermodynamic equilibrium (or Gibbs state). This equilibrium can be defined as the probability law with density

(1.5)

M^{N}_{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}},R}(X_{N},V_{N}):=\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}^{N}_{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}},R}}\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{|v_{i}|^{2}}{2}-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\mathscr{U}_{s,R}\left(\frac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}}\right)\right)

at positions
X_{N}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})
and velocities
V_{N}=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{N})
. The term
\mathscr{Z}^{N}_{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}},R}
is a normalization constant such that

\int M_{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}},R}^{N}\,\mathrm{d}X_{N}\,\mathrm{d}V_{N}=1.

We want to understand the fluctuation field
\zeta_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{t}
around the equilibrium: for a test function
g
, we define

(1.6)

\zeta_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{t}(g):=\sqrt{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}g({\mathbf{x}}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}_{i}(t),{\mathbf{v}}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}_{i}(t))-\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}g({\mathbf{x}}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}_{i}(t),{\mathbf{v}}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}_{i}(t))\right]\right).

In the previous equality,
({\mathbf{x}}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}_{i}(t),{\mathbf{v}}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}_{i}(t))
denotes the position and velocity of the
i
-th particle at time
t
, and the expectation is taken with respect to the Gibbs measure
M_{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}},R}^{N}\,\mathrm{d}{X_{N}}\,\mathrm{d}{V_{N}}
. The fluctuation field
\zeta_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{t}
has been studied by Bodineau
et al
in the hard sphere system. They have shown
\zeta_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{t}
verifies a central limit theorem, and converges to a Gaussian field
\zeta_{0}^{t}
described by the linearized Boltzmann equation (see
[
BGSR17
,
BGSRS23b
,
BGSRS24
,
LB25
]
). Our study is focused on the description of the covariance for more general interaction potential.

One can now write a first informal version of the theorem proved in the present paper (a rigorous version is written in Theorem
3
2
2

2

The vague Claims
1
and
2
are written for simplicity of the presentation in the
canonical
setting,
id est
the number of particles is a constant
N
that goes to infinity. In fact, we will rather work in the
grand canonical
setting, where the number of particles is a random variable
\mathscr{N}
, with
\mathbb{E}\mathscr{N}
going to infinity. The system becomes less rigid, and it avoids the apparition of additional error terms. However, one can expect that the canonical and grand canonical systems behave asymptotically in the same way (although the proof is missing).

).

Claim 1
.

Consider a system of
N
particles evolving with respect to Newton’s laws, interacting through the pairwise potential
\mathscr{U}_{s,R}(\cdot/{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}})
, with
s\geq 1

. At time zero, the particles are distributed according to the Gibbs equilibrium measure.
Parameters
N,{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}},R
tuned as

N\to\infty,\penalty\ R\to\infty,\penalty\ R=o\left((\log\log N)^{1/6}\right),\penalty\ {\rm and}\penalty\ N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(\log R)^{-1}&\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ s=1,\\
1&\penalty\ {\rm if\penalty\ }s>1.\end{array}\right.

Fix
g
and
h
two test functions. Then denoting
M(v):=\frac{e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}
, we have that

(1.7)

\mathbb{E}_{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}}\left[\zeta_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{t}(g)\zeta_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{0}(h)\right]\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\int{\mathbf{g}}(t,x,v)h(x,v)M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{x}\,\mathrm{d}{v}

with
{\mathbf{g}}(t,x,v)
the solution of the linearized equation

(1.8)

\left\{\begin{split}\partial_{t}{\mathbf{g}}+v\cdot\nabla_{v}{\mathbf{g}}&=\mathscr{L}_{\infty}{\mathbf{g}},\\
{\mathbf{g}}(t=0,x,v)&=g(x,v)\end{split}\right.\penalty\ {\rm where}\penalty\ \mathscr{L}_{\infty}{\mathbf{g}}:=\left\{\begin{split}\frac{1}{M}\left(Q_{L}(Mg,M)+Q_{L}(M,Mg)\right)&\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ s=1,\\
\frac{1}{M}\left(Q_{s}(Mg,M)+Q_{s}(M,Mg)\right)&\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ s>1.\end{split}\right.

1.1.
Modification of the scaling parameters and the sub-Coulomb case (
s\in[0,1)
)

For a fix
s\geq 1
and a cut-off function
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to[0,1]
, we define the
interaction radius

\varepsilon:={R}\,{{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}}
, the
coupling constant

\alpha=R^{-s}

and the interaction potential

\mathscr{V}(x):=\frac{\chi(|x|)}{|x|^{s}}.

Hence, we have the equality
\mathscr{U}_{s,R}(x/{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}})=\alpha\mathscr{V}(x/\varepsilon)
.

The scaling parameters
(\varepsilon,\alpha)
and
({\varepsilon_{\rm eff}},R)
are two different parameterizations of the same system, and taking

N\to\infty,\penalty\ R\to\infty,\penalty\ R=o\left((\log\log N)^{1/6}\right),\penalty\ {\rm and}\penalty\ N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(\log R)^{-1}&\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ s=1\\
1&\penalty\ {\rm if\penalty\ }s>1\end{array}\right.

is equivalent to taking

N\to\infty,\penalty\ \alpha\to 0,\penalty\ \frac{1}{\alpha}=o\left((\log\log N)^{s/12}\right),\penalty\ {\rm and}\penalty\ N\varepsilon^{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\alpha^{-2}|\log\alpha|^{-1}&\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ s=1\\
\alpha^{-2/s}&\penalty\ {\rm if\penalty\ }s>1\end{array}\right.

In the core of the proof (from Section
2
to the end), we prefer using
(\varepsilon,\alpha)
as it will simplify the notation. However, the couple
({\varepsilon_{\rm eff}},R)
may be more natural to describe a gas interacting
via
a power law
\mathscr{U}_{s}
,
s\geq 1
.

Another advantage of taking the parameters
(\varepsilon,\alpha)
is that it allows a natural generalization of the result to potentials in the sub-Coulomb range
s\in[0,1)
which is the microscopic interpretation of the so-called
grazing collision limit.

We look at a system of
N
particles interacting pairwise
via
the potential
\alpha\mathscr{V}(\cdot/\varepsilon)
. At time
t=0
, they are distributed with respect to the canonical Gibbs measure

M^{N}_{\varepsilon,\alpha}(X_{N},V_{N}):=\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}_{N,\varepsilon,\alpha}}\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{|v_{i}|^{2}}{2}-\alpha\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\mathscr{V}\left(\frac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right),

where
\mathscr{Z}_{N,\varepsilon,\alpha}
is a normalization constant, and
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
the fluctuation field (defined in the same way as
\zeta_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}
). Then we can write an informal theorem (with the rigorous version written in Theorem
3
):

Claim 2
.

Consider a system of
N
particles evolving according to Newton’s laws, interacting through the pairwise potential
\alpha\mathscr{\mathscr{V}}(\cdot/\varepsilon)
, where
s\in[0,1)
. At time zero, the particles are distributed with respect to the equilibrium measure.
Parameters
N,\varepsilon,\alpha
are tuned as

N\to\infty,\penalty\ \alpha\to 0,\penalty\ \tfrac{1}{\alpha}=o((\log\log N)^{1/12}),\penalty\ {\rm and}\penalty\ N\varepsilon^{2}=\alpha^{-2}.

Fix
g
and
h
two test functions. Then, denoting
M(v):=\frac{e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}
,

(1.9)

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(g)\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(h)\right]\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\int{\mathbf{g}}(t,x,v)h(x,v)M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{x}\,\mathrm{d}{v}

with
{\mathbf{g}}(t,x,v)
the solution of the linearized equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{t}{\mathbf{g}}+v\cdot\nabla_{v}{\mathbf{g}}=\frac{c_{\mathscr{V}}}{M}\Big(Q_{L}(Mg,M)+Q_{L}(M,Mg)\Big),\\
{\mathbf{g}}(t=0,x,v)=g(x,v),\end{array}\right.

where
c_{\mathscr{V}}
is a diffusion constant defined by

(1.10)

\displaystyle c_{\mathscr{V}}=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\delta(k\cdot\vec{e}_{1})|k|^{2}|\hat{{\mathscr{V}}}(k)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{k}

where
\vec{e}_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
an unitary vector and
\hat{{\mathscr{V}}}(k)
is the Fourier transform of
{\mathscr{V}}
using the convention

\hat{{\mathscr{V}}}(k)=\int\mathscr{V}(x)e^{-ik\cdot x}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Remark 1.2
.

The Landau equation is usually derived from interacting particle systems in the
weak coupling limit
: one fixes

\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/2},\penalty\ \penalty\ N\varepsilon^{3}=1.

For example, Bobylev, Pulvirenti, and Saffirio provided in
[
BPS13
]
a consistency result (a result at time
0
) for smooth interaction in this scaling.

In the present article, we are only able to treat the cases

\alpha^{-1}=o(\log|\log\varepsilon|)^{1/12}\ll\varepsilon^{-1/2},\penalty\ \penalty\ N\varepsilon^{2}\alpha^{2}=1,

which are far from the weak coupling limit. The same Landau equation is, however, expected to hold in all intermediate regimes connecting Boltzmann-Grad and weak-coupling scalings (see on that subject
[
NSV18
,
NVW22
,
NVW21
,
PS21
]
).

1.2.
State of the art

Now we recall some results about the derivation of the Boltzmann and Landau equation from a particle system
.

In the nonlinear setting, the only results hold for potentials
\mathscr{U}(\cdot)
supported in a ball
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\,|x|\leq R\}
. In the Boltzmann-Grad scaling
N{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=1
, the distribution of a typical particle follows the Boltzmann equation up to a time
O(1/R^{2})
. The first derivation was performed by Lanford
[
Lan75
]
for hard spheres (
i.e.

\exp(-\mathscr{U}_{\rm hs}(r))=\mathbbm{1}_{r>1}
) and King
[
Kin75
]
for more general compactly supported potentials (see also
[
GSRT13
,
PSS14
,
Den18
,
BGSRS18
]
). The previous results have two defects. They are valid only up to a small time (for the atmosphere at temperature
20^{\circ}\!{\rm C}
and pressure
10^{5}{\rm Pa}
, the validity time is
10^{-9}s
), and the results apply only to a compactly supported interaction potential. A first long-time result out of equilibrium is
[
IP89
]
, in a setting where the dispersive effects are dominant. More recently, Deng, Hani and Ma provide a long time result out of equilibrium
[
DHM24
]
for more general initial datum
f_{0}
. Their result holds up to the minimum between the existing time of
f(t)
, the solution of the Boltzmann equation with initial data
f_{0}
, and a time
O(\log|\log N|^{\alpha})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
given by a sampling time strategy.

For the Landau equation, the unique results hold only at time
0
(see
[
BPS13
,
Win21
,
Due21
]
): the authors obtain the equality

(\partial_{t}f)_{|t=0}=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{0}+Q_{L}(f_{0},f_{0}).

Note that in
[
BPS13
,
Win21
]
, the authors do not look at the real particle system but at a simplified version (they truncate the BBGKY hierarchy). In
[
Due21
]
, Duerinckx proves the consistency of the Lenard–Balescue equation, which can be understood as a modification of the Landau equation. However, the scaling is far from the collisional scaling that we are treating here.

This is not the first attempt to derive a linear version of the Boltzmann or Landau equations.

A linear equation can be obtained from the study of the
Lorentz gas
: one fixes a background of obstacles distributed with respect to the Poisson measure of parameter
N
. One follows a unique particle that interacts pairwise with the obstacles
via
the potential
\mathscr{U}(\cdot/{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}})
with
N\varepsilon^{2}=1
. In the limit
N\to\infty
, the density of the tagged particle
f(t,x,v)
follows the linear Boltzmann equation

(1.11)

\partial_{t}f(t,x,v)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f(t,x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\left(f(t,x,|v|\eta)-f(t,x,v)\right)b(\eta\cdot\tfrac{v}{|v|})\,\mathrm{d}\eta

where
b
is a collision kernel. It does not depend on the norm of
v
as the obstacles are fixed. This system was first described by Gallavotti
[
Gal69
]
in the case of the hard spheres, and later adapted for the derivation of the linear Boltzmann equation without cutoff
[
DP99
]
and linear Landau equation
[
DR01
]
. Note that in
[
DP99
]
, the authors used, in the same way as this paper, interaction through a screened potential
\tilde{\mathscr{U}}(x)=\mathbbm{1}_{|x|\leq R}\left(|x|^{-s}-R^{-s}\right)
for
s>1
.

A second possibility is the treatment of the
linear particle setting
. We look at a system of
N
interacting particles, initially distributed with respect to the Gibbs measure (
1.5
). Then, one wants to follow a tagged particle of the system, id est compute the covariance (for
g
,
h
two test functions)

(1.12)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}\left[h({\mathbf{x}}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}(t),{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}(t))g({\mathbf{x}}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}(0),{\mathbf{v}}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}(0))\right]=\int h(x,v){\mathbf{g}}(t,x,v)M(v)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}x

where
{\mathbf{g}}(t,x,v)
is solution of the linear Boltzmann equation

(1.13)

\partial_{t}g(t,x,v)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}g(t,x,v)=\frac{1}{M(v)}Q_{\mathscr{U}}(Mg,M),

where
Q_{\mathscr{U}}
is the Boltzmann operator associated with the potential
\mathscr{U}
. The first long-time result on such a system was obtained in
[
vBLLS80
]
. Later, Bodineau
et al.

[
BGSR17
]
provided a quantitative proof in the hard sphere setting. This proof has been adapted to other potentials in order to derive the linear Landau equation
[
Cat18
]
or the linear Boltzmann equation without cutoff
[
Ayi17
]
. In her paper, Ayi does not consider interactions through the cut-off potential
\mathscr{U}_{s,R}
, but directly a long-range potential
\mathscr{U}
with fast decay at infinity (she needs
\mathscr{U}(r)\leq O(\exp(-\exp\exp|x|^{4}))
). Up to our knowledge, it is the unique result where the particles interact through genuine infinite potential.

While the linear setting is a
O(1)
perturbation of equilibrium, the linearized setting (which is treated in the present paper) is a
O(N)
perturbation of equilibrium. In
[
Spo81
,
Spo83
]
, Spohn showed that the fluctuation field
\zeta_{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}
formally verifies a Central Limit Theorem in the limit
N\to\infty
. The first step to prove such a theorem is to compute the covariance of the fluctuation field. This has been performed in the hard sphere setting by Bodineau
et al.
in
[
BGSR17
]
in dimension 2 and
[
BGSRS23b
]
in any dimension bigger than
3
(note also
[
LB25
]
). Then, they proved that the limit is Gaussian in
[
BGSRS24
]
.

2.
Definition of the system and strategy of the proof

2.1.
The Hamiltonian dynamics

Let
{\mathbb{T}}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(with
d\geq 2
) be the domain. We denote
\mathbb{D}={\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
its tangent bundle and
\mathbb{D}^{n}
the
n
-particle canonical phase space. In the following, we use the notation

X_{n}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{T}^{n},\penalty\ V_{n}=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{nd},\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ z_{i}=(x_{i},v_{i})\in\mathbb{{D}}.

On each
\mathbb{D}^{n}
, we construct the Hamiltonian dynamics associated with the Energy

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{n}(Z_{n}):=\frac{1}{2}|V_{n}|^{2}+\mathscr{V}_{n}(X_{n}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathscr{V}_{n}(X_{n}):=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\alpha\mathscr{V}\left(\frac{|x_{i}-x_{j}|}{\varepsilon}\right),

(2.2)

\displaystyle\forall i\in[1,n],\Bigg\{{\begin{split}\\[3.0pt]
&\tfrac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}x_{i}=\nabla_{v_{i}}\mathscr{H}_{n}(Z_{n}(t))=v_{i},\\[-3.0pt]
&\tfrac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}{t}}v_{i}=-\nabla_{x_{i}}\mathscr{H}_{n}(Z_{n}(t))=\frac{\alpha}{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\nabla\mathscr{V}\left(\frac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{\varepsilon}\right).\end{split}}

We impose the following condition on the interaction potential

Assumption 2.1
.

There exists a constant
s\in[0,\infty)
and a decreasing cut-off function
\chi\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty))\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}([0,1))
such that

(2.3)

\mathscr{V}(x):=\frac{\chi(|x|)}{|x|^{s}},\penalty\ \chi(0)=1,\penalty\ \chi([1,\infty))=\{0\}.

This dynamics is well defined for all times, almost everywhere in
\mathbb{D}^{n}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

2.2.
Grand-canonical ensemble and stationary measure

In the following, we choose not to fix the number of particles
\mathscr{N}
, and suppose that
\mathscr{N}
is a random variable with
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}[\mathscr{N}]
going to infinity when
\varepsilon
goes to
0
(we say that we consider the
grand canonical ensemble
). It will simplify a lot of computations
3
3

3

The treatment of the canonical setting remains an open problem.
.

We denote
\mathscr{D}:=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\mathbb{D}^{n}
the grand canonical phase space. We can then extend the Hamiltonian dynamics to
\mathscr{D}
and denote
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}(t)
the realization (defined almost surely) of the Hamiltonian flow on
\mathscr{D}
with random initial data
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}(0)
: for
\mathscr{N}=n
,
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}(t)
follows the Hamiltonian dynamics on
\mathbb{D}^{n}
.

The initial data are sampled according to the stationary measure introduced now. The
grand canonical Gibbs measure

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}
(and its expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}
) are defined on
\mathscr{D}
as follows: an application
H:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a test function if there exists a sequence
(h_{n})_{n\geq 0}
with
h_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{n})
and

\text{if}\penalty\ \mathscr{N}=n,\penalty\ {\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}=({\mathbf{z}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathbf{z}}_{n}),\penalty\ H({\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}):=h_{n}({\mathbf{z}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathbf{z}}_{n}).

Fixing
\mu>0
the
chemical potential
4
4

4

It takes the role of the number of particle
N
has in the canonical setting.
, we define
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}
as

(2.4)

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}[H({\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}})]:=\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}}\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{\mu^{n}}{n!}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{n}}h_{n}(Z_{n})\,\frac{e^{-\mathscr{H}_{n}(Z_{n})}}{(2\pi)^{nd/2}}\,\mathrm{d}{Z_{n}},

where
\mathscr{Z}
is a normalisation constant such that
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}[1]=1
. The
mean free path

\mathfrak{d}
is defined by

(2.5)

\mathfrak{d}=\frac{1}{\mu\,\varepsilon^{d-1}}.

It can be interpreted as the typical distance crossed by a particle between two collisions.

Figure 1.
The first particle will meet the second one. Here
v
is of order
1
.

We will consider only
\varepsilon
small and
\mathfrak{d}\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{=}O(1)
, for which the series defining the Gibbs measure converges absolutely.

The empirical distribution at time
t
is defined as the average configuration of particles at time
t
: for any
g
test function on
\mathbb{D}
,

(2.6)

\pi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(g):=\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathscr{N}}g({\mathbf{z}}_{i}(t)).

At equilibrium, we have the following law of large numbers. Denote

(2.7)

M(v):=\frac{e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}.

Proposition 2.2
.

For any continuous and bounded test function
g:{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and for any
\delta>0
,

(2.8)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}\left[\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}^{t}(g)-\int g(z)M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z}\right|\geq\delta\right]=0.

Remark 2.1
.

The previous result is a simple corollary of the Lanford theorem and of the stationarity in time of the measure (see
[
Lan75
,
Kin75
]
). From Proposition
4.1
, we can deduce the
L^{2}
counterpart of this law of large numbers.

The aim of this article is to investigate the next order, namely the
fluctuation field
5
5

5

As the Gibbs measure is stationary in time, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}[\pi_{0}^{\varepsilon}(g)]=\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}[\pi^{t}_{\varepsilon}(g)]
.

(2.9)

\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(g):=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\pi_{\varepsilon}^{t}(g)-\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}[\pi_{0}^{\varepsilon}(g)]\Big).

2.3.
Binary collision, scattering, and definition of the linearized Boltzmann operator

Interactions involving more than two particles become negligible in the Boltzmann-Grad limit.

The present section is dedicated to describing the map between pre-collisional and post-collisional velocities. It is called the
scattering map
(see Chapter 8 of
[
GSRT13
]
for a more detailed discussion).

Consider two interacting particles
1
and
2
following the Hamiltonian dynamic associated with
\mathscr{H}_{2}
. At time
0
, particles have coordinates
(X_{2}(0),V_{2}(0))
with

x_{1}(0)=\varepsilon\nu,\penalty\ x_{2}(0)=0,\penalty\ v_{1}=v\penalty\ {\rm and}\penalty\ v_{2}(0)=v_{*}

where
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
(v-v_{*})\cdot\nu>0
.

The particles will interact on a finite interval
[0,[\tau]]
with
[\tau]
the infimum of
\{\tau>0,\,|x_{2}(\tau)-x_{1}(\tau)|>\varepsilon\}
. The time
[\tau]
is finite and bounded by
\frac{\varepsilon|v_{1}-v_{2}|}{|\nu\times(v_{1}-v_{2})|^{2}}

, with
\times
the cross product (see Lemma
B.1
). We define
(\nu^{\prime},v^{\prime},v_{*}^{\prime})
as

\nu^{\prime}:=\frac{x_{2}(\tau)-x_{1}(\tau)}{\varepsilon}{\rm\penalty\ and\penalty\ }(v^{\prime},v_{*}^{\prime}):=\big(v_{1}([\tau]),v_{2}([\tau])\big).

In addition, the scattering conserves both momentum, kinetic energy, and angular momentum:

(2.10)

v+v_{*}=v^{\prime}+v_{*}^{\prime},\penalty\ |v|^{2}+|v_{*}|^{2}=|v^{\prime}|^{2}+|v^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}{\rm\penalty\ and\penalty\ }(v-v_{*})\times\nu=(v^{\prime}-v^{\prime}_{*})\times\nu^{\prime}

We deduce that

(2.11)

|(v-v_{*})\cdot\nu|=|(v^{\prime}-v^{\prime}_{*})\cdot\nu^{\prime}|.

Definition 2.1
.

The
scattering application
defined by

(2.12)

\xi_{\alpha}:(\nu,v,v_{*})\mapsto(\nu^{\prime},v^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{*})

is a local diffeomorphism which sends the measure
\,\mathrm{d}{v}\!\,\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\!\,\mathrm{d}\nu
to
\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}\!\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}_{*}}\!\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\prime}
. In addition, it does not depend on the particle radius
\varepsilon
.

Figure 2.
The scattering between two particles.

We define the linearized Bolzmann operator in the King’s form:

(2.13)

\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{U}}g(v):=\int_{\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(g(v^{\prime})+g(v_{*}^{\prime})-g(v)-g(v_{*})\big)((v-v_{*})\cdot\nu)_{+}M(v_{*})\,\mathrm{d}\nu\,\,\mathrm{d}{v_{*}},

where we apply the scattering with interaction potential
\mathscr{U}(\cdot)
, and
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}:=\mathscr{L}_{\alpha\mathscr{V}}
.

This operator describes the variation of mass in a gas
due to changes of velocity of colliding particles. It is well known
that the operator
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}
is a self-adjoint non-positive operator on
L^{2}(M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z})
.

Remark 2.2
.

We say that the Boltzmann operator
\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{U}}
has a cutoff because we truncate the long range interaction.

There is another interpretation of this property. For parameters
(v,v_{*},\nu)
, we can define the vector
\eta
such that

v^{\prime}=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}+\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\eta,\penalty\ v_{*}^{\prime}=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}-\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\eta,

and
b_{\alpha}(v-v_{*},\eta)
(called the
collision kernel
) the Jacobian of the application
\nu\mapsto\eta
:

((v-v_{*})\cdot\nu)_{+}\,\mathrm{d}\nu\to b_{\alpha}(v-v_{*},\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

We say that the Boltzmann operator has a cutoff because for any
v-v_{*}
, the following bound holds

\int b_{\alpha}(v-v_{*},\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta<\infty.

See Chapter 8 of
[
GSRT13
]
, Appendix of
[
PSS14
]
and Proposition 2.3.2 of
[
LB23
]
for explicit estimations of the collision kernel
b_{\alpha}
for certain class of interaction potential.

2.4.
Convergence to the linearized Boltzmann equation with a cut-off

We recall that we have divided the proof of Claim
1
into two steps. The first step is the Boltzmann-Grad limit
\mu\to\infty
. As we want to take the
grazing collision
limit
\alpha\to 0
in a second step, we need a quantitative rate of convergence.

We define the norm

(2.14)

\|g\|_{0}:=\sup_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{D}}\left|M^{-1}(v)g(x,v)\right|\penalty\ {\rm and}\penalty\ \|g\|_{k}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|\nabla^{\alpha}g\|_{0}.

Theorem 1
.

Let
g
and
h
be two test functions
\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{D})
, with
\|g\|_{1},\|h\|_{1}<\infty
. Then there exist three constants
C>1
,
C^{\prime}>1
and
\mathfrak{a}\in(0,1)
depending only on the dimension such that for any
\varepsilon
small enough,
T>1
,
\theta<\tfrac{1}{C^{\prime}T^{2}}
,
\alpha,\mathfrak{d}\in(\log|\log\varepsilon|^{-1},1)
,

(2.15)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\bigg|\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\zeta^{t}_{\varepsilon}(h)\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(g)\Big]-\int h(z){\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}(t,z)M(z)\,\mathrm{d}z\bigg|\leq C\left(\frac{\theta T^{2}}{\mathfrak{d}^{3}}+\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\left(\frac{CT}{\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{2^{\frac{T}{\theta}+1}}\right)\|g\|_{1}\|h\|_{1},

where
{\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}(t,z)
is the solution in
L^{\infty}(\,\mathrm{d}t,L^{2}(M(v)\,\mathrm{d}z))

of the linearized Boltzmann equation

(2.16)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}{\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}(t)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}{\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}(t)&=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{d}}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}{\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}(t),\\
{\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}(t=0)&=g\end{split}

Remark 2.3
.

It is classical that there is a unique solution to the linearized Boltzmann equation, which is bounded globally in time in
L^{2}(M(v)\,\mathrm{d}z)
(see e.g., Section 7 in
[
CIP94
]
).

The theorem is valid in any dimension
d\geq 2
. Its proof is the main purpose of the present article. We conclude the proof of Theorem
1
by Estimation (
3.26
), and we outlined the main step of the proof in Section
2.6
.

2.5.
Derivation of the linearized Landau equation and Boltzmann equation without cut-off

In this section, we discuss the second step, namely the grazing collision limit. We fix the dimension at
d=3
as it is the physical case.
We only state the main results, as the proof can be found in the joint article
[
LBW22
]
.

The case where the singularity
\tfrac{1}{r^{s}}
of the potential is stronger than the Coulomb singularity (
s>1
).

In the limit
\alpha\to 0
, we will only see the effects of the singularity at the origin. We define the power law potential
\mathscr{U}_{s}(r):=1/r^{s}
. It is natural to guess that one has convergence of the Boltzmann operators

(2.17)

\alpha^{-\frac{2}{s}}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{U}_{s}}

which is a linearized Boltzmann operator without cutoff (see Appendix
A
for a rigorous definition of
\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{U}_{s}}
and a justification of the scaling
\mathfrak{d}=\alpha^{2/s}
).

Remark 2.4
.

We say that the Boltzmann operator
\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{U}_{s}}
has no cutoff because particles can interact at long range, and the collision kernel
b_{s}(v-v_{*},\eta)
associated to the potential
1/r^{s}
(defined in Remark
2.2
) is not integrable in the
\eta
variable (see (
1.2
)).

The Coulomb case
s=1
.

It is not possible to define the Boltzmann operator for the Coulomb potential. However, we can prove (see
[
LBW22
]
) that for
g
a test function smooth enough,

(2.18)

\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}|\log{\alpha}|}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}g\underset{\alpha\to 0}{\longrightarrow}c_{\mathscr{V}}\mathscr{K}g

where
c_{\mathscr{V}}=1
is a diffusion constant and
\mathscr{K}
is the linearized Landau operator

(2.19)

{\mathscr{K}}g(v)=\frac{2\pi}{M(v)}\nabla_{v}\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{P^{\perp}_{v-v_{*}}}{|v-v_{*}|}(\nabla g(v)-\nabla g(v_{*}))M(v)M(v_{*})\,\mathrm{d}{v_{*}}\right).

Treat now the weak singularity
s\in[0,1)
.

For these potentials, the scaling and the diffusion constant change:

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}=\alpha^{2},\penalty\ \penalty\ 2\pi c_{\mathscr{V}}=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\delta(k\cdot\vec{e}_{1})|k|^{2}|\hat{{\mathscr{V}}}(k)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{k},

where
\vec{e}_{1}
is a unit vector, and we use the convention
\hat{{\mathscr{V}}}(k)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-ik\cdot x}{\mathscr{V}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}{x}
for the Fourier transform of
{\mathscr{V}}
. Then

(2.21)

\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}g\underset{\alpha\to 0}{\longrightarrow}c_{\mathscr{V}}\mathscr{K}g

The previous discussion can be summarized by the following theorem:

Theorem 2

(L.B.-Winter,
[
LBW22
]

)
.

For
g:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
smooth and
\mathscr{V}
respecting Assumption
2.1
, there exists a positive constant
C
such that

(2.22)

\left\|\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}g-\mathscr{L}_{\infty}g\right\|_{L^{2}(M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z})}\leq\frac{C}{|\log\alpha|}\|g\|_{3},

where
\mathscr{L}_{\infty}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}
are given by

Singularity

Mean free-path

Limiting operator

s>1

\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}:=\alpha^{2/s}

\mathscr{L}_{\infty}=\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{U}_{s}}

s=1

\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}:=\alpha^{2}|\log\alpha|

\mathscr{L}_{\infty}=\mathscr{K}

0\leq s<1

\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}:=\alpha^{2}

\mathscr{L}_{\infty}=c_{\mathscr{V}}\mathscr{K}
,

\displaystyle c_{\mathscr{V}}=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\delta(k\cdot\vec{e}_{1})|k|^{2}|\hat{{\mathscr{V}}}(k)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{k}

In addition, defining
{\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t)
the solution of

(2.23)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}{\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}{\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t)&=\mathscr{L}_{\infty}{\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t),\\
{\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t=0)&=g\end{split}

and
{\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}
the solution of (
2.16
) with
\mathfrak{d}:=\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}
, the following convergence holds

(2.24)

{\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}\underset{\alpha\to 0}{\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}}{\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}\penalty\ {\rm in}\penalty\ L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{+}(L^{2}(M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z})).

Combining it with Theorem
1
we obtain the main theorem:

Theorem 3
.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z})
be two test functions.

Consider a potential
\mathscr{V}
such that the Assumptions
2.1
are verified and
\mathscr{V}(r)\underset{r\to 0^{+}}{\sim}\frac{1}{r^{s}}
,
s\geq 0

.

Fix the scaling
\mu\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}=1
.
Then we have the following convergence result: for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(h)\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(g)\right]\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\\
\alpha\to 0\\
\alpha\log|\log\varepsilon|^{\frac{\tilde{s}}{6}}\to\infty\end{subarray}}{\longrightarrow}\int h(z){\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t,z)M(z)\,\mathrm{d}z

where
\tilde{s}=\max(s,1)
and
{\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t)
is the solution of the equation (
2.23
).

Remark 2.5
.

We recall that in the sup-Coulomb case (
s>1
), taking the limit

\varepsilon\to 0,\penalty\ \alpha\to 0,\penalty\ \mu\,\varepsilon^{2}=\mathfrak{d}^{-1}_{s,\alpha}=\alpha^{-2/s}\text{ and }\alpha\log|\log\varepsilon|^{\frac{s}{6}}\to\infty

is equivalent to taking the limit

{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}\to 0,\penalty\ R\to\infty,\penalty\ \mu\,{\varepsilon_{\rm eff}}^{2}=1\text{ and }\frac{\log|\log\varepsilon|^{\frac{1}{6}}}{R}\to\infty.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

First, the space
E:=\{g:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R},\penalty\ \left\|g\right\|_{1}<\infty\}
is dense in
L^{2}(M(v\,\mathrm{d}{z}))
.

Since the two bilinear operators

\displaystyle(h,g)\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(h)\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(g)\right],\penalty\ (h,g)\mapsto\int h(z){\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t,z)M(z)\,\mathrm{d}z

are continuous on
L^{2}(M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z})
(see
[
BGSRS23b
]
), it is sufficient to take
g,h\in E
.

Set
T:=\max(1,t)
. Fixing
\theta:=\frac{1}{\beta\log|\log\varepsilon|}
for
\beta\in(0,1)
small enough,

C\left(C\frac{T^{2}\theta}{\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}^{3}}+\frac{T}{\theta}2^{T^{2}/\theta^{2}}\left(\frac{CT}{\mathfrak{d}_{s,\alpha}}\right)^{2^{T/\theta}}\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}/2}\right)=o(1)

Hence Theorem
1
provides

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(g)\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(h)\right]=\int h(z){\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}(t,z)M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z}+o(\|g\|_{1}\|h\|_{1}).

Theorem
2
provides the convergence

\int h(z){\mathbf{g}}_{\alpha}(t,z)M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z}\to\int h(z){\mathbf{g}}_{\infty}(t,z)M(v)\,\mathrm{d}{z}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Notations.
For
\omega\subset\mathbb{N}
a finite subset and
r\leq|\omega|
, we denote
\mathscr{P}^{r}_{\omega}
the set of the unordered partitions
(\rho_{1},\cdots,\rho_{r})
of the set
\omega
.

For
m<n
two integers, we denote
[m,n]:=\{m,m+1,\cdots,n\}
and
[n]:=[1,n]
.

For
Z_{n}\in\mathbb{D}^{n}
, and
\omega\subset[n]
, we denote

Z_{\omega}:=(z_{\omega(1)},\cdots,z_{\omega(|\omega|)})

where
\omega(i)
is the
i
-th element of
\omega
counted in increasing order.

Given a family of particles indices
\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{n}\}
, the notation
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n})
indicates the ordered sequence in which
\forall k\neq l
,
i_{k}\neq i_{l}
. In addition,

•

{\underline{i}}_{n}:=(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n})
,

•

for
m\leq n
,
{\underline{i}}_{m}=(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})
, and more generally for
\omega\subset[1,n]
,
{\underline{i}}_{\omega}:=(i_{\min\omega},\cdots,i_{\max\omega})
,

•

for
0\leq m<n
and
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})
,
\underset{(i_{m+1},\cdots,i_{n})}{\sum}
denotes the sum over every family
(i_{m+1},\cdots,i_{n})
such that for
1\leq k<l\leq n
,
i_{k}\neq i_{l}
, and

\underset{{\underline{i}}_{n}}{\sum}=\underset{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n})}{\sum},

•

{\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}:=({\mathbf{z}}_{i_{1}},\cdots,{\mathbf{z}}_{i_{n}})
, as an ordered sequence.

We also precise the Landau
6
6

6

from Edmund Landau and not Lev Landau.
notation:
A=B+O(D)
means that there exists a constant
C
depending only on the dimension such that
|A-B|<C\,D
. We denote
A\lesssim B
if
A=O(B)
.

When we perform estimations,
C
is a positive constant depending only on the dimension (which can change from one line to another), and the final time
t
is supposed to be bigger than
1
(in general, we prefer to denote
\tau
any intermediate time).

Finally, let
h_{n}
be a function on
\mathbb{D}^{n}
. We denote (by a slight abuse of notation)

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\big[h_{n}\big]:=\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\mu^{n}}\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n})}h_{n}\big({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}\big)\Bigg]

and the associated centered function defined on
\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}

\hat{h}_{n}({\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}):=\frac{1}{\mu^{n}}\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n})}h_{n}\big({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}\big)-\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\big[h_{n}\big].

2.6.
Strategy of the proof of Theorem
1

The general strategy of proof of Theorem
1
is inspired by
[
BGSRS23b
,
LB25
,
LB23
]
, in which the hard sphere system is studied. It is known, however, that the treatment of smooth potentials in the low-density limit leads to a number of delicate complications and methodology. This is already true for the law of large numbers, see eg
[
GSRT13
,
PSS14
]
where the proof of
[
Kin75
]
was completed and extended. The difficulties are due to the fact that interactions are not instantaneous. On the other hand, the extension to a long time of (
2.15
) for hard spheres, achieved in
[
LB25
]
(the short-time version was known long ago in
[
Spo83
]
) involves a different class of difficulty and technology. The main task of this paper is to push much further all the above-mentioned techniques in such a way as to allow the grazing collision limit
\alpha\to 0
. This combination is nontrivial, and we outline the main features and novelties involved to achieve the result.

As
\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(g)
is a mean-free random variable on
\mathscr{D}
, we can write

(2.25)

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\zeta^{t}_{\varepsilon}(h)\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\mathscr{N}}h({\mathbf{z}}_{i}(t))\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(g)\right].

We see that the function
h
is evaluated at time
t
, whereas the function
g
is evaluated at time
0
. The first step of the proof is the construction of a family of functionals
(\Phi_{1,n})_{1\leq n}
,
\Phi_{1,n}^{t}:L^{\infty}(\mathbb{D})\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{n})
such that for any initial configuration
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}\in\mathscr{D}
,

(2.26)

\sum_{i=1}^{\mathscr{N}}h({\mathbf{z}}_{i}(t))=\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h]({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}(0)).

The first part of Section
3
is dedicated to giving an explicit expression to the
\Phi_{1,n}^{t}
,
via
a
dynamical cluster development
(introduced by Sinai
[
Sin72
]
in a different setting and later by Bodineau
et al.
for hard spheres in Boltzmann-Grad scaling (see
[
BGSRS22
]
)). If the particle
1
does not meet any other particles, it moves along a straight line. We deduce that

\Phi_{1,1}^{t}[h](x_{1},v_{1})=h(x_{1}+tv_{1},v_{1}).

If the particle
1
meets only the particle
2
, then the trajectory of
1
(denoted
{\mathsf{z}}_{1}(t,\text{$1$ and $2$ interact})
) is governed by the Hamiltonian equation associated to the energy

\frac{|{\mathsf{v}}_{1}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|{\mathsf{v}}_{2}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\mathscr{V}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{1}-{\mathsf{x}}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right),

with initial conditions
({\mathsf{z}}_{1}(0),{\mathsf{z}}_{2}(0))=(z_{1},z_{2})
. In order to write
\Phi_{1,2}^{t}[h](z_{1},z_{2})
, one also needs to compensate for the term
\Phi_{1,}^{t}[h](z_{1})
which appears now to be an error. Note that in this error term, the particles
1
and
2
do not interact. One writes

({\mathsf{z}}_{1},{\mathsf{z}}_{2})(t,\text{$1$ and $2$ do not interact}):=(x_{1}+tv_{1},v_{1},x_{2}+tv_{2},v_{2}).

We deduce that

\Phi_{1,2}^{t}[h](z_{1},z_{2})=\left[h({\mathsf{z}}_{1}(t,\text{$1$ and $2$ interact})-h({\mathsf{z}}_{1}(t,\text{$1$ and $2$ do not interact}\right])\mathbbm{1}_{\text{$1$ and $2$ meet}}.

In order to generalize this construction, we define the pseudotrajectory
{\mathsf{Z}}_{n}(t,\text{history})
(for the moment in a vague sense, see Definition
3.3
for a proper definition) as a trajectory of
n
particles where some couple of particles interacts and some couple of particles ignores each other. The "histories" are combinatorial parameters describing which couple of particle interacts (see Remark
3.4
). We denote
\mathfrak{H}_{n}
the set of histories (it will be precisely defined in Remark
3.4
). We finally obtain

(2.27)

\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h](Z_{n}):=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\sum_{{\rm history}\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}}h({\mathsf{z}}_{1}(t,Z_{n},{\rm history}))\mathbbm{1}_{\rm history}\sigma({\rm history})

In the preceding formula,
\mathbbm{1}_{\rm history}
requires that the pseudotrajectory is possible (if we have all the needed collisions), and
\sigma({\rm history})=\pm 1
is a sign.

Figure 3.
Exemple of pseudotrajectory for four particles.

Remark 2.6
.

This representation differs from the one used in
[
Kin75
,
GSRT13
,
PSS14
]
, which is based on the Grad’s representation of the BBGKY hierarchy
[
Gra49
]
. It leads to a drastic improvement to the argument in
[
LB25
]
(see below and Remark
3.8
).

Following the classical derivation of the Boltzmann equation (and here of the linearized Boltzmann equation), there will be two main steps. First, we need to prove that each term of the expansion in (
2.26
)

(2.28)

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h]({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}(0))\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right]

converges to its formal limit. In the limit, the dynamics loses its Hamiltonian character, and particles become punctual (see Section
6
).

The main obstacles to this convergence are
multiple encounters

(interactions between more than three particles) and
recollisions
. A recollision can be defined (a more proper definition will be given in Definition
3.7
)
as a meeting (with interaction or with overlap) between two particles
q
and
\bar{q}
, beginning at time
\tau
and such that we can find a sequence of couples of particles
(q=q_{1},\bar{q}_{1}),(q_{2},\bar{q}_{2})\cdots(q_{r},\bar{q}_{r}=\bar{q})
, such that
q_{i+1}\in\{q_{i},\bar{q}_{i}\}
and that
q_{i}
and
\bar{q}_{i}
meet before time
\tau
.
Recollisions and multiple encounters
become rare in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
(quantitative estimations are performed in Section
B.2
).

The second step is an
a priori
bound of the terms of the series (
2.26
). To improve estimates to a longer time interval than the one obtained in
[
GSRT13
,
PSS14
]
, it is convenient to consider
L^{2}
estimates (see
[
BGSR17
,
BGSRS23b
,
BGSRS24
,
LB25
]
). Indeed, because
\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(g)
is a mean-free random variable, for any intermediate time
t_{s}\in[0,t]
,

\begin{split}\Bigg|\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Bigg[\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}\Phi_{1,n}^{t-t_{s}}[h]({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}(t_{s}))\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(g)\Bigg]\Bigg|&=\Big|\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\mu^{n-\frac{1}{2}}\hat{\Phi}_{1,n}^{t-t_{s}}[h]({\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}(t_{s}))\zeta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(g)\Big]\Big|\\[-7.0pt]
&\leq\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\mu^{2n-1}\left(\hat{\Phi}_{1,n}^{t-t_{s}}[h]({\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}(t_{s}))\right)^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\left(\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(g)\right)^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\mu^{2n-1}\left(\hat{\Phi}_{1,n}^{t-t_{s}}[h]({\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}(0))\right)^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\Big[\left(\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(g)\right)^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

using a Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and the invariance of the Gibbs measure. Hence, it is possible to start a development along pseudotrajectories and stop at time
t_{s}
when they become "pathological". We say that pseudotrajectories become pathological when their number explodes (this notion will be made precise after (
2.33
) and is linked to recollisions and multiple encounters).
Then we can ignore what happens in the time interval
[0,t_{s}]
.

We need to bound the
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\big[\big(\hat{\Phi}_{1,n}^{t}[h]\big)^{2}\big]
, which are based on the estimations of terms of type

(2.29)

\sup_{l\leq n}\penalty\ \frac{n^{l}}{\mu^{l-1}}\int\left|{\Phi}_{1,n}^{t-t_{s}}[h](Z_{n}){\Phi}_{1,n}^{t-t_{s}}[h](Z_{[n-l,2n-l]})\right|e^{-\mathscr{H}_{2n-l}(Z_{2n-m})}\,\mathrm{d}{Z_{2n-m}},

see Proposition
4.1
.

Remark 2.7
.

An
L^{\infty}\to L^{1}
estimation is used in the classical derivation of the Boltzmann equation (see
[
Lan75
,
Kin75
,
GSRT13
,
PSS14
]
). It is valid only for short times. The linear version of the problem (one tagged particle followed in a background initially at equilibrium) is only a
O(1)
perturbation of equilibrium in
L^{\infty}
. Thus, the
L^{1}
bounds are valid for all time (see
[
vBLLS80
,
BGSR16
,
Ayi17
,
Cat18
]
). The linearized setting is a
O(\mu)
perturbation of the equilibrium, and
L^{1}
bounds are no longer sufficient to reach long time out of equilibrium (Spohn used them to describe the fluctuations on short time in
[
Spo81
]
).

Unfortunately, we do not know how to efficiently take into account the sign
\sigma({\rm history})
in the bound of
\Phi_{1,n}^{t-t_{s}}
. Thus, we resort to using the naive bound

(2.30)

\left|\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h]\right|\lesssim\frac{\|h\|}{(n-1)!}\sum_{{\rm history}\in\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\rm history}.

where
\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{H}_{n}
is a set of parameters that is a little bit smaller than
\mathfrak{H}_{n}
(it will be made precise in Remark
3.4
). Two particles with initial coordinates
(x_{1},v_{1})
and
(x_{2},v_{2})
can collide on the time interval
[0,t]
if and only if
x_{2}
is in a cylinder of radius
\varepsilon
and length
t|v_{1}-v_{2}|
. We deduce that

(2.31)

\int\mathbbm{1}_{\text{$1$ and $2$ collide on $[0,t]$}}e^{-\mathscr{H}_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}Z_{2}\lesssim t\varepsilon^{d-1}\lesssim\frac{t}{\mu\mathfrak{d}}.

For a pseudotrajectory involving
n
particles, there are at least
n-1
collisions (all particles are linked by a chain of collisions). Using a generalization of the preceding argument, we deduce the following
L^{1}
bound

(2.32)

\int\sum_{{\rm history}\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm history}\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{\prime}}e^{-\mathscr{H}_{n}}\,\mathrm{d}Z_{n}\lesssim n^{n}\left(\frac{t}{\mu\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{n-1}

for some
C
(a rigorous proof is provided in Proposition II.6 of
[
BGSRS22
]
7
7

7

The estimation is provided in
[
BGSRS22
]
in the hard sphere setting but should be generalized to more general interaction. We will not need this precise estimation, and we will limit ourselves to proving for a given history

\int\mathbbm{1}_{\rm history}e^{-\mathscr{H}_{n}}\,\mathrm{d}Z_{n}\lesssim\left(\tfrac{Cnt}{\mu\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{n-1}.

). As we perform
L^{2}
estimates, we will encounter terms of the form
\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm history}_{1}}(Z_{n})\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm history}_{2}}(Z_{n})
where
({\rm history}_{1},{\rm history}_{2})\in(\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{\prime})^{2}
are two different histories. The problem is that the geometry of the set
\{Z_{n}|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm history}_{1}}(Z_{n})=1\}
is quite complicated and we do not know a better estimate than the naive bound

\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm history}_{1}}(Z_{n})\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm history}_{2}}(Z_{n})\leq\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm history}_{1}}(Z_{n}).

From this, we may obtain the bound on (
2.29
) bigger than

(2.33)

\frac{n^{n}}{\mu^{n-1}}\int\left|\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h]\right|^{2}e^{-\mathscr{H}_{n}}\,\mathrm{d}Z_{n}\lesssim{\|h\|}\left(\frac{Ct}{\mu^{2}\mathfrak{d}}\right)^{n-1}\left|\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{\prime}\right|.

Thus, we are led to counting the number of "histories",
id est
, the number of pseudotrajectories involving
n
particles. The set
|\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{\prime}|
is too large (it is of order
O((Cn)^{n})
for some constant
C>0
, see Remark
3.4
) to allow the series
\sum_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[(\hat{\Phi}_{1,n}^{t})^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}
to converge. We would need that
|\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{\prime}|
is of order
O(C^{n})
for some constant
C>0
. To overcome this problem, we will perform the pseudotrajectory decomposition only until an intermediate time
t_{s}
, such that the set of collision parameters needed
\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
remains controllable. We observe that there are two reasons for the set of parameters to explode: the
pathology
(recollisions or multiple meetings) and an uncontrolled number of particles
n
.

We now introduce two samplings, one to control regular collisions and one to control recollisions and multiple encounters
.

The first sampling has a relatively large step
\theta:=\tfrac{1}{\beta\log|\log\varepsilon|}
(for some constant
\beta
large enough). We stop the pseudotrajectory development at time
t-k\theta
if there are more than
2^{k}
particles involved in the pseudotrajectory. Hence, the number of particles at time
0
remains controlled. This sampling follows
[
BGSR16
,
BGSR17
]
8
8

8

Note that in
[
Fou24
]
, the author improved the sampling strategy in the linear setting in order to obtain a better convergence rate than (
2.15
).
.

The second sampling has a shorter step,
\delta:=\varepsilon^{1/12}
. We stop the expansion at time
t_{s}:=t-k\delta
if the pseudotrajectory has at least one recollision on
[t_{s},t]
(but no recollision on
[t_{s}+\delta,t]
). Imposing recollisions creates an additional geometric condition, and thus, an extra-smallness gain.

Unfortunately, we still have too many possible histories. In order to reduce their number, we follow the idea of
[
LB25
]
and
separate the pseudotrajectories into two categories. In
non-pathological
pseudotrajectories, there is no recollision nor multiple encounters
on the time interval
[t_{s},t_{s}+\delta]
.
We are in a setting close to the case without recollision, and we only need
C^{n}
parameters (
C
a fixed constant) to describe the histories.

We explain now how to treat the pathological recollisions part. The initial data
{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\mathscr{N}}(0)
is conditioned such that on each interval
[k\delta,(k+1)\delta]
a particle can
encounter
9
9

9

The meaning of
encounter
will be precise in Definition
3.5
.
with only a finite number of particles
\gamma
(we will take
\gamma:=12d
). Hence, for a pseudotrajectory
{\rm z}_{1}(t,Z_{n},{\rm history})
, the history has to describe first a partition of
[n]
into small clusters of particles that interact together on
[0,\delta]
and how they really interact. As the size of each cluster is uniformly bounded, the number of histories is at most of order
C^{n}
for some
C>1
.

Figure 4.
An example of one pathological pseudotrajectory (on the left) and a non-pathological one (on the right)

The paper is organised as follows:

•

In Section
3
, we give a proper definition of histories, and we use it to construct the functionals
\Phi_{1,n}^{t}
. Then we implement the two samplings. This allows us to decompose
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{t}(h)\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(g)\right]
into a main term, plus error terms.

•

In Section
4
, we introduce standard
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon})
estimates that are necessary to bound terms of the form
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\big|\hat{\Psi}\big|^{2}
(for some symmetric function
\Psi_{n}:\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
) by the integrals

\forall m\leq n,\penalty\ \int\left|{\Psi}_{n}(Z_{n}){\Psi}_{n}(Z_{[n-m,2n-m]})\right|e^{-\mathscr{H}_{2n-m}(Z_{2n-m})}\,\mathrm{d}{Z_{2n-m}}.

The proof is an adaptation of Section 3 of
[
BGSRS23b
]
, and is based on static cumulant decompositions.

•

Section
5
is dedicated to the bound of the pseudotrajectory development
\Phi_{1,n}^{0}
in the case where recollisions and multiple meetings are forbidden. This allows us to treat the pseudotrajectory with many particles.

•

Section
6
is dedicated to the convergence of the main term.

•

Section
7
is dedicated to the treatment of the non-pathological recollisions.

•

Section
8
is dedicated to the treatment of pathological recollisions and of multiple meetings.

•

Annex
B
is dedicated to the analyses of trajectories leading to recollisions of multiple encounters
. We use a strategy similar to the one of
[
PSS14
]
. It has the advantage of giving estimations independent of the form of the potential
\mathscr{V}
(supposing it respects Assumption
2.1
).

3.
Development along pseudotrajectories and time sampling

3.1.
Dynamical cluster development

For any test functions
h
and
g:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, we want to compute

\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\zeta^{t}_{\varepsilon}(h)\zeta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(g)\right]=\frac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\mathscr{N}}h({\mathbf{z}}_{i}(t))\sum_{j=1}^{\mathscr{N}}g({\mathbf{z}}_{j}(0))\right].

We have a sum evaluated at time
t
and a sum evaluated at time
0
. In order to compute it, we have to pull back the second sum to time
0
: we want to construct a family of applications
\Phi_{1,n}^{t}:L^{\infty}{(\mathbb{D})}\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{n})
such that for almost all initial data
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}(0)\in\mathscr{D}

h({\mathbf{z}}_{i_{1}}(t))=\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{(i_{2},\cdots,i_{n})}\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h]({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}(0)).

More generally, we will construct a family of functional
\Phi_{m,n}^{t}:L^{\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{m})\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{n})
(with
m<n
) such that for any test functions
h_{m}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{D}^{m})
,

h_{m}({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{m}}(t))=\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{(i_{m+1},\cdots,i_{n})}\Phi_{m,n}^{t}[h_{m}]({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}(0)).

Remark 3.1

(Comparison with the hard sphere setting)
.

In the hard spheres setting, a tree pseudotrajectories development is used as it comes directly from the BBGKY hierarchy (see, for example,
[
Lan75
,
PS15
,
BGSRS23b
,
LB25
]
). We begin at time
0
with
n
particles, and at each collision, we can remove or not remove one particle to end at time
t
with
m
particles. However, in the case of physical potential, writing the BBGKY hierarchy is difficult as particles can overlap, and there can be interaction between more than three particles (see
[
Gra49
,
Kin75
,
GSRT13
,
PSS14
]
for a description of the BBGKY hierarchy). Hence, we will use a different kind of pseudotrajectory development called “dynamical cluster development” (see
[
Sin72
,
PSW16
,
BGSRS22
]
, from which we take inspiration).

Figure 5
.
On the left a tree pseudotrajectory, on the right a graph pseudotrajectory.

Fix
\lambda\subset\mathbb{N}
a finite set of particles. We denote by
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(\tau)=(\mathsf{X}^{\lambda}(\tau),\mathsf{V}^{\lambda}(\tau))
the Hamiltonian trajectory, linked to the energy

\mathscr{H}_{\lambda}(Z_{\lambda}):=\sum_{q\in\lambda}\frac{|v_{q}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q,\bar{q}\in\lambda\\
q\neq\bar{q}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{V}\left(\frac{x_{q}-x_{\bar{q}}}{\varepsilon}\right)

of the particles
\lambda
(isolated of the other particles) with initial data
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(0)=Z_{\lambda}
. For any subset
\lambda^{\prime}\subset\lambda
, we denote
{\mathsf{Z}}_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{\lambda}(\tau)
the trajectory of particles
\lambda^{\prime}
in
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(\tau)
.

Definition 3.1
.

Given
Z_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{D}^{|\lambda|}
, we construct the graph
G
with vertex
\lambda
and
(q,\bar{q})\in\lambda^{2}
is an edge if and only if
q<\bar{q}
and if there exists a time
\tau\in[0,t]
such that

\exists\tau\in[0,t],\penalty\ \left|{\mathsf{x}}^{\lambda}_{q}(\tau)-{\mathsf{x}}^{\lambda}_{\bar{q}}(\tau)\right|\leq\varepsilon.

We say that
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(\tau)
forms a
dynamical cluster
if the graph
G
is connected. We denote
\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda|}(Z_{\lambda})
the indicator function that the trajectory
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(\tau)
forms a dynamical cluster.

In the same way, for
\omega\subset\lambda
, we say that
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(\tau)
forms a
\omega
-cluster
if, in the collision of
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(\tau)
, all the particles are in the same connected components of
G
that one of the particles of
\omega
. The function
\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda|}^{\omega}(Z_{\lambda})
is equal to
1
if
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(\tau)
is a
\omega
-cluster,
0
else.

Remark 3.2
.

In the following, we consider that all the graphs are unoriented.

Definition 3.2
.

We say that trajectories
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda}(\tau)
and
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\tau)
(with
\lambda\cap\lambda^{\prime}=\emptyset
) have a
dynamical overlap
if there exists a couple of particles
(q,{q}^{\prime})\in\lambda\times\lambda^{\prime}
and some time
\tau\in[0,t]
, such that
|\mathsf{x}_{q}^{\lambda}(\tau)-\mathsf{x}_{{q}^{\prime}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\tau)|\leq\varepsilon
. Then we denote
\lambda\overset{\circ}{\sim}\lambda^{\prime}
.

For
(Z_{\lambda_{1}},\cdots,Z_{\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}}})\in\prod_{i=1}^{{\mathbf{l}}}\mathbb{D}^{|\lambda_{i}|}
initial data, we look at the indicator function that for any
i\neq j
,
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda_{i}}(\tau)
and
{\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda_{j}}(\tau)
have no dynamical overlap. We can expand it as

(3.1)

\begin{split}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq{\mathbf{l}}}\big(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{i}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\lambda_{j}}\big)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\subset[1,l]\\
1\in\omega\end{subarray}}&\underset{:=O\hskip-5.0ptO_{|\omega|}(Z_{\lambda_{1}},Z_{\lambda_{\omega(2)}},\cdots,Z_{\lambda_{\omega(|\omega|)}})}{\underbrace{\sum_{C\in\mathscr{C}(\omega)}\prod_{(i,j)\in E(C)}-\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{i}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\lambda_{j}}}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j)\in([{\mathbf{l}}]\setminus\omega)^{2}\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\big(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{i}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\lambda_{j}}\big).\\
&\end{split}

We have defined
(O\hskip-5.0ptO_{l})_{l}
as the cumulants of the dynamical overlap indicator functions.

We make a partition of
\mathscr{D}
depending on the way particles interact during the time interval
[0,t]
: fixing
\mathscr{N}\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\underline{i}}_{m}
, (we recall that
\mathscr{P}_{\omega}^{r}
is the set of partitions into
r
subsets of
\omega
)

h_{m}({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{m}}(t))=\sum_{{\mathbf{l}}=1}^{\mathscr{N}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{i}}_{m}\subset\lambda_{1}\\
(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}})\in\mathscr{P}_{[\mathscr{N}]\setminus\lambda_{1}}^{{\mathbf{l}}-1}\end{subarray}}h_{m}({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{m}}(t))\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta^{{\underline{i}}_{m}}_{\lambda_{1}}({\mathbf{Z}}_{\lambda_{1}})\prod_{i=2}^{{\mathbf{l}}}\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda_{i}|}({\mathbf{Z}}_{\lambda_{i}})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq{\mathbf{l}}}\big(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{i}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\lambda_{j}}\big)\\
=\sum_{{\mathbf{l}}=1}^{\mathscr{N}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{i}}_{m}\subset\lambda_{1}\\
(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}})\in\mathscr{P}_{[\mathscr{N}]\setminus\lambda_{1}}^{{\mathbf{l}}-1}\end{subarray}}h_{m}({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{m}}(t))\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta^{{\underline{i}}_{m}}_{\lambda_{1}}({\mathbf{Z}}_{\lambda_{1}})\prod_{i=2}^{{\mathbf{l}}}\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda_{i}|}({\mathbf{Z}}_{\lambda_{i}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\subset[{\mathbf{l}}]\\
1\in\omega\end{subarray}}O\hskip-5.0ptO_{|\omega|}({\mathbf{Z}}_{\underline{\lambda}_{\omega}})\\[-5.0pt]
\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j)\in(\omega\setminus[{\mathbf{l}}])^{2}\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\big(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{i}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\lambda_{j}}\big).

We make the change of variables

\left({\mathbf{l}},\left(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}}\right),\omega\right)\mapsto\left(\rho,{\mathbf{l}}_{1},\left(\bar{\lambda}_{1},\cdots,\bar{\lambda}_{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}\right),{\mathbf{l}}_{2},\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\lambda}_{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}\right)\right)

where

\displaystyle\rho:=\bigcup_{i\in\omega}\lambda_{i},\,{\mathbf{l}}_{1}:=|\omega|,\,{\mathbf{l}}_{2}:={\mathbf{l}}-|\omega|,\,\bar{\lambda}_{1}:=\lambda_{1},

\displaystyle\left(\bar{\lambda}_{2},\cdots,\bar{\lambda}_{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}\right):=(\lambda_{j})_{j\in\omega\setminus\{1\}}{\rm\,and\,}\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\lambda}_{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}\right):=(\lambda_{j})_{j\in[{\mathbf{l}}]\setminus\omega}.

The set
\rho
is the set of particles linked to
{\underline{i}}_{m}

via
a chain of interactions or overlaps. We get that
h_{m}({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{m}}(t))
is equal to

\sum_{\rho\supset{\underline{i}}_{m}}\sum_{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}=1}^{|\rho|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{i}}_{m}\subset\bar{\lambda}_{1}\subset\rho\\
(\bar{\lambda}_{2},\cdots,\bar{\lambda}_{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}})\in\mathscr{P}_{{\rho\setminus\bar{\lambda}_{1}}}^{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}-1}\end{subarray}}h_{m}\left({\mathsf{Z}}^{\bar{\lambda}_{1}}_{{\underline{i}}_{m}}(t)\right)\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta^{{\underline{i}}_{m}}_{\bar{\lambda}_{1}}\left({\mathbf{Z}}_{\bar{\lambda}_{1}}\right)\prod_{i=2}^{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\bar{\lambda}_{i}|}\left({\mathbf{Z}}_{\bar{\lambda}_{i}}\right)O\hskip-5.0ptO_{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}\!\left({\mathbf{Z}}_{\bar{\lambda}_{1}},\cdots,{\mathbf{Z}}_{\bar{\lambda}_{{\mathbf{l}}_{1}}}\right)\\[-10.0pt]
\times\sum_{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}=1}^{\mathscr{N}-|\rho|}\sum_{(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\lambda}_{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}})\in\mathscr{P}^{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}_{[\mathscr{N}]\setminus\rho}}\prod_{i=1}^{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}}\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}|}({\mathbf{Z}}_{\tilde{\lambda}_{i}})\!\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j)\in([\mathscr{N}]\setminus\omega)^{2}\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\!\!\big(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\tilde{\lambda}_{j}}\big).

The second line is the sum over all possible partitions
(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\lambda}_{{\mathbf{l}}_{2}})
of
[\mathscr{N}]\setminus\rho
of the indicator function that they are effectively the dynamical cluster of the initial data. Hence, it is equal to one. We identify the
n
-th
dynamical cumulant
as

(3.2)

\Phi^{t}_{m,n}[h_{m}](Z_{n}):=\frac{1}{(n-m)!}\sum_{{{\mathbf{l}}}=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[m]\subset\lambda_{1}\subset[n]\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{\mathbf{l}})\\
\in\mathscr{P}^{{\mathbf{l}}-1}_{[n]\setminus\lambda_{1}}\end{subarray}}h_{m}({\mathsf{Z}}^{\lambda_{1}}_{[m]}(t))\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda_{1}|}^{[m]}(Z_{\lambda_{1}})\prod_{i=2}^{{\mathbf{l}}}\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda_{i}|}(Z_{\lambda_{i}})\\[-10.0pt]
\times O\hskip-5.0ptO_{l}(Z_{\lambda_{1}},\cdots,Z_{\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}}}),

and we can now write the dynamical cluster expansion:

Proposition 3.1
.

Fix a family of particle
{\underline{i}}_{m}
. For almost all
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathscr{N}}\in\mathscr{D}
, we have

(3.3)

h_{m}\left({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{m}}(t)\right)=\sum_{n\geq m}\sum_{(i_{m+1},\cdots,i_{n})}\Phi^{t}_{m,n}[h_{m}]\left({\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}(0)\right).

Figure 6.
Example of trajectory in a dynamical cumulant. We want to follow the particles
\{1,2,3,4\}
.

Definition 3.3

(First type of pseudotrajectory)
.

In the following, for a given
m\leq n
,
\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}})
a partition of
[n]
, we denote
{\mathsf{Z}}(t,Z_{n},\underline{\lambda})
the trajectory of the
n
particles following the Hamiltonian dynamics linked to

\mathscr{H}_{\underline{\lambda}}(Z_{n}):=\sum_{\ell=1}^{{\mathbf{l}}}\mathscr{H}_{\lambda_{\ell}}(Z_{\lambda_{\ell}}).

We define now the notion of collision graph:

Definition 3.4

(collision graph)
.

Fix
m\leq n
, collision parameters
\underline{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}})
and an initial position
Z_{n}\in\mathbb{D}^{n}
.

We construct the
collision graph
with vertex
[n]
and with labeled edges of the form
(i,j)_{\tau,s}
,
\tau\in[0,t]
,
s\in\{\pm 1\}
. The edges
(i,j)_{\tau,s}
is in the graph if

•

\tau\in(0,t)
,
\left|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\tau,\underline{\lambda})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(\tau,\underline{\lambda})\right|=\varepsilon
,
\left({\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\tau,\underline{\lambda})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(\tau,\underline{\lambda})\right)\cdot\left({\mathsf{v}}_{i}(\tau,\underline{\lambda})-{\mathsf{v}}_{j}(\tau,\underline{\lambda})\right)>0
,

•

or
\tau=0
,
\left|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0,\bar{\lambda})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(0,\bar{\lambda})\right|<\varepsilon
,

•

s=1
if
i
and
j
are in the same
\lambda_{k}
,
s=-1
else.

Figure 7.
The collision graph associated with the pseudotrajectory of Figure
6
.

Remark 3.3
.

Fix
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{\ell})
a partition of
[n]
. Using Penrose’s tree inequality (see
[
Pen63
,
BGSRS20
,
Jan
]
), the cumulant function
O\hskip-5.0ptO_{n}(Z_{\lambda_{1}},\cdots,Z_{\lambda_{n}})
is bounded by

(3.4)

\left|O\hskip-5.0ptO_{n}(Z_{\lambda_{1}},\cdots,Z_{\lambda_{n}})\right|\leq\sum_{T\in\mathscr{T}([\ell])}\prod_{(i,j)\in E(T)}\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{i}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\lambda_{j}}

where
\mathscr{T}([\ell])
is the set of simply connected graph on
[\ell]
. The case of equality is reached, so we cannot expect a good
L^{\infty}
bound of
O\hskip-5.0ptO_{n}
.

Remark 3.4
.

We will describe the set
\{\text{history}\}
that has been used in Section
2.6
for the decomposition of
\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h]
:
{\rm history}
is a triplet
({\mathbf{l}},(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}}),C)\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}
, where the set of history
\mathfrak{H}_{n}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{n}:=\left\{({\mathbf{l}},(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}}),C)\big|{\mathbf{l}}\in[1,n]\,;\,\{1\}\subset\lambda_{1}\subset[n]\,;\,(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}})\in\mathscr{P}_{\lambda_{1}^{c}}^{{\mathbf{l}}-1}\,;\,C\in\mathscr{C}([{\mathbf{l}}])\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\rm history}:=\prod_{i=1}^{{\mathbf{l}}}\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda_{i}|}({\mathbf{Z}}_{\lambda_{i}})\prod_{(i,j)\in E(C)}\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{i}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\lambda_{j}}

\displaystyle\sigma({\rm history}):=(-1)^{|E(C)|},

where
|E(C)|
is the number of edges of the graph
C
. One has

\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h](Z_{n}):=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\sum_{{\rm history}\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}}h({\mathsf{z}}^{\lambda_{1}}_{1}(t,Z_{\lambda_{1}})\mathbbm{1}_{\rm history}\sigma({\rm history}).

Using the Penrose’s tree inequality (
3.4
), one obtains

\left|\Phi_{1,n}^{t}[h](Z_{n})\right|\leq\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\sum_{{\rm history}\in\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}_{n}}h({\mathsf{z}}^{\lambda_{1}}_{1}(t,Z_{\lambda_{1}})\mathbbm{1}_{\rm history}\sigma({\rm history})

where
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{H}_{n}
is a smaller set of parameters

\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}_{n}:=\left\{({\mathbf{l}},(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}}),C)\big|{\mathbf{l}}\in[1,n]\,;\,\{1\}\subset\lambda_{1}\subset[n]\,;\,(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}})\in\mathscr{P}_{[n]\setminus\lambda_{1}}^{{\mathbf{l}}-1}\,;\,C\in\mathscr{T}([{\mathbf{l}}])\right\}.

Using that
|\cup_{{\mathbf{l}}}\mathscr{P}^{{\mathbf{l}}}_{[n]}|\leq e^{n}
,
|\mathscr{T}([{\mathbf{l}}])|={\mathbf{l}}^{{\mathbf{l}}-2}
,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}_{n}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{{{\mathbf{l}}}=1}^{n}\left|\left\{({\mathbf{l}},(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}}))\big|{\mathbf{l}}\in[1,n]\,;\,\{1\}\subset\lambda_{1}\subset[n]\,;\,(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}})\in\mathscr{P}_{[n]\setminus\lambda_{1}}^{{\mathbf{l}}-1}\right\}\right|\times\left|\mathscr{T}([{\mathbf{l}}])\right|,

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{{\mathbf{l}}}=1}^{n}C^{n}\times{\mathbf{l}}^{{\mathbf{l}}-2}\leq C^{n}n^{n}

for some constant
C>0
, which is faster in
n
than any geometric sequence. This shows that estimations of the form (
2.33
) cannot be useful yet.

3.2.
Conditioning

We describe now the conditioning used to control the pathological recollisions, that were introduced in Section
2.6
.

Definition 3.5

(Possible cluster)
.

Let
Z_{r}\in\mathbb{D}^{r}
an initial configuration. Consider
\omega_{1},\cdots,\omega_{p}
a family of subsets of
[r]
such that

\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}\omega_{i}=[r],

and
(\underline{\lambda}_{i})_{i\leq p}=(\lambda_{i}^{1},\cdots,\lambda_{i}^{l_{i}})_{i\leq p}
where each
\underline{\lambda}_{i}
is a partition of the corresponding
\omega_{i}
. We denote
\mathscr{G}_{i}
the collision graph of the pseudotrajectory
{\mathsf{Z}}(\tau,Z_{\omega_{i}},\underline{\lambda}_{i})
on the time interval
[0,\delta]
. The graph
\mathscr{G}
is the merge of all the
\mathscr{G}_{i}
.

We say that
Z_{r}
forms a
possible cluster
if there exists a couple
((\omega_{i})_{i}
,
(\underline{\lambda}_{i})_{i})
such that the graph
\mathscr{G}
is connected.

Definition 3.6

(Definition of the set
\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}
)
.

Let
\gamma>0
be a fixed integer,
\delta>0
a time scale, and
\mathbb{V}>0
a velocity bound. We construct
\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathscr{D}
the set of particle configurations such that for any time
\tau\in\{0,\delta,2\delta,\cdots,

t\}
, there is no possible cluster of size bigger than
\gamma
at time
\tau
, and inside any subset of particles
\omega\subset[1,\mathscr{N}]
with less than
\gamma
elements,
\tfrac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{V}}_{\omega}(\tau)\|^{2}
is bounded by
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{V}^{2}
.

We have the following bound on the measure of the complement of
\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}
:

Proposition 3.2
.

There exists a constant
C_{\gamma}
depending only on
\gamma
and on the dimension such that

(3.5)

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}\left(\Upsilon^{c}_{\varepsilon}\right)\leq C_{\gamma}\frac{t}{\delta}\left(\mu\delta^{\gamma}+\mu^{\gamma}e^{-\mathbb{V}^{2}/4}\right).

Proof.

We take the notation of the definition
3.5
.

Suppose that
Z_{r}
(with
r>\gamma
) forms a possible cluster. We want to show that there exists a subset
\varpi\subset[r]
such that
Z_{\varpi}
forms a possible cluster and
\gamma+1\leq|\varpi|\leq 2\gamma+2
. As
Z_{r}
forms a possible cluster, there exists a couple
((\omega_{i})_{i},(\underline{\lambda}_{i})_{i})
such that the graph
\mathscr{G}
defined in Definition
3.6
is connected. We define
\mathscr{G}_{\tau}
as the subgraph of
\mathscr{G}
with edges

\left\{(q,\bar{q})_{(\tau^{\prime},\sigma)}\in E(\mathscr{G}),\tau^{\prime}<\tau\right\}.

We consider
\tau
the infimum of
\{\tau^{\prime}>0,\mathscr{G}_{\tau^{\prime}}\text{ is connected}\}
. Then, the graph
\mathscr{G}_{\tau}
has exactly two connected components. One of them, denoted
\varpi
, verifies
\lceil\tfrac{r}{2}\rceil\leq|\varpi|\leq r-1
. Iterating the procedure, we obtain the expected result.

We deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}\Big(

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}^{c}\Big)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{t/\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\left[\sum_{n=\gamma+1}^{2(\gamma+1)}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n})}\mathbbm{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{Z}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}(k\delta)\text{\,form\,a}\\
{\rm possible\,cluster}\end{subarray}}+\sum_{n=1}^{\gamma}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n})}\mathbbm{1}_{\|{\mathbf{V}}_{{\underline{i}}_{n}}(k\delta)\|\geq\mathbb{V}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{\delta}\left(\sum_{n=\gamma+1}^{2(\gamma+1)}\frac{1}{n!}\mu^{n}\int\mathbbm{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{n}\text{\,form\,a\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }\\
{\rm possible\,cluster}\end{subarray}}M^{\otimes n}\,\mathrm{d}Z_{n}+\sum_{n=1}^{\gamma}\frac{1}{n!}\mu^{n}\int\mathbbm{1}_{\|V_{k^{\prime}}\|\geq\mathbb{V}}\,M^{\otimes n}\,\mathrm{d}Z_{n}\right).

Using that (see Lemma
B.3
)

(3.6)

\int\mathbbm{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{n}\text{\,form\,a\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }\\
{\rm possible\,cluster}\end{subarray}}M^{\otimes n}\,\mathrm{d}Z_{n}\leq C_{\gamma}\mu^{-n+1}\delta^{n-1},

(3.7)

\int\mathbbm{1}_{\|V_{k^{\prime}}\|\geq\mathbb{V}}\,M^{\otimes n}\,\mathrm{d}Z_{n}\leq C_{n}e^{-\frac{\mathbb{V}^{2}}{4}}

we obtain the expected result.

We used that the Gibbs measure is time invariant.
∎

Hence, if we fix
\delta:=\varepsilon^{1/12}
,
\mathbb{V}:=|\log\varepsilon|
and fix
\gamma=24d
,
\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}(\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}^{c})
is
O(\varepsilon^{d})
.

3.3.
The main part of the cumulant

We define three kinds of pathology for the pseudotrajectories.

Definition 3.7
.

Fix
m\leq n
, collision parameters
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}})
and an initial position
Z_{n}\in\mathbb{D}^{n}
.

•

There is an
overlap
if there are two particles
q,q^{\prime}
and a time
\tau\in\delta\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,t]
such that
|{\mathsf{x}}_{q}(\tau)-{\mathsf{x}}_{q^{\prime}}(\tau)|\leq\varepsilon
.

•

Fix a time
\tau
and particles
i_{i},\cdots i_{k}
. We define a graph
G^{\tau}
with vertex
\{i_{1},\cdots i_{k}\}
, and where
(i_{a},i_{b})
is an edge if and only if

\Big|{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{a}}(\tau,\underline{\lambda})-{\mathsf{x}}_{i_{b}}(\tau,\underline{\lambda})\Big|\leq\varepsilon.

There is a
multiple encounter

between
i_{i},\cdots i_{k}
at time
\tau
if
G^{\tau}
is connected.

•

Fix
Z_{n}\in\mathbb{D}^{n}
such that there is not a multiple encounters
during
[0,t]
.

We say that there is a
recollision
if the collision graph has a cycle.

These pathological terms will be considered as error terms, and we forbid them in the main part of our development:
we define
\Phi^{0,t}_{m,n}
as the part of
\Phi^{t}_{m,n}
with only non-pathological pseudotrajectories

\begin{array}[]{r@{}l}\Phi^{0,t}_{m,n}[h_{m}](Z_{n})&:=\frac{1}{(n-m)!}\sum_{{{\mathbf{l}}}=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[m]\subset\lambda_{1}\subset[n]\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{\mathbf{l}})\\
\in\mathscr{P}^{{\mathbf{l}}-1}_{[n]\setminus\lambda_{1}}\end{subarray}}h_{m}({\mathsf{Z}}_{[m]}(t,\underline{\lambda}))O\hskip-5.0ptO_{l}(Z_{\lambda_{1}},\cdots,Z_{\lambda_{{\mathbf{l}}}})\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\times\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda_{1}|}^{[m]}(Z_{\lambda_{1}})\prod_{i=2}^{{\mathbf{l}}}\Delta\hskip-6.0pt\Delta_{|\lambda_{i}|}(Z_{\lambda_{i}})\mathbbm{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{no\penalty\ pathology}\end{subarray}}\lx@intercol.\end{array}

Forgetting the pathological cases allows us to consider a simpler parametrization of the pseudotrajectory. We denote
G
as the collision graph. The graph
G^{\prime}
is constructed by removing the edges
(i,j)_{\tau,s}
where
i
and
j
are in
[m]
. The edges of
G^{\prime}
can be ordered:
(i_{k},j_{k})_{\tau_{k},s_{k}}
with
\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\cdots<\tau_{n-m}
(the
\tau_{i}
are disjoint for almost all initial data).
